1.  **Level 0:** Start node.

    *   Visited: `{L}`

2.  **Level 1:** Neighbors of L.
    *   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.
    *   L is connected to M. Path: `[L, M]`
    *   L is connected to E. Path: `[L, E]`
    *   Queue: `[(M, [L, M]), (E, [L, E])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, M, E}`

3.  **Level 2:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 1.
    *   Dequeue `(M, [L, M])`.
        *   M is connected to K. Path: `[L, M, K]`
        *   M is connected to J. Path: `[L, M, J]`
    *   Dequeue `(E, [L, E])`.
        *   E is connected to F. Path: `[L, E, F]`
        *   E is connected to B. Path: `[L, E, B]`
    *   Queue: `[(K, [L, M, K]), (J, [L, M, J]), (F, [L, E, F]), (B, [L, E, B])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, M, E, K, J, F, B}`

4.  **Level 3:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 2.
    *   Dequeue `(K, [L, M, K])`.
        *   K is connected to X. Path: `[L, M, K, X]`
        *   K is connected to F (already visited via E, but this path is longer).
    *   Dequeue `(J, [L, M, J])`.
        *   J is connected to I. Path: `[L, M, J, I]`
        *   J is connected to P. Path: `[L, M, J, P]`
    *   Dequeue `(F, [L, E, F])`.
        *   F is connected to X (already visited via K, but this path is longer).
        *   F is connected to G. Path: `[L, E, F, G]`
    *   Dequeue `(B, [L, E, B])`.
        *   B is connected to H. Path: `[L, E, B, H]`
        *   B is connected to C. Path: `[L, E, B, C]`
        *   B is connected to N. Path: `[L, E, B, N]`
    *   Queue: `[(X, [L, M, K, X]), (I, [L, M, J, I]), (P, [L, M, J, P]), (G, [L, E, F, G]), (H, [L, E, B, H]), (C, [L, E, B, C]), (N, [L, E, B, N])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, M, E, K, J, F, B, X, I, P, G, H, C, N}`

5.  **Level 4:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 3.
    *   Dequeue `(X, [L, M, K, X])`.
        *   X is connected to G. Path: `[L, M, K, X, G]` (G already visited via F, but this path is longer).
    *   Dequeue `(I, [L, M, J, I])`.
        *   I is connected to H. Path: `[L, M, J, I, H]` (H already visited via B, but this path is longer).
        *   I is connected to C. Path: `[L, M, J, I, C]` (C already visited via B, but this path is longer).
        *   I is connected to P (already visited via J, but this path is longer).
    *   Dequeue `(P, [L, M, J, P])`.
        *   P is connected to D. Path: `[L, M, J, P, D]`
        *   P is connected to O. Path: `[L, M, J, P, O]`
        *   P is connected to Y. Path: `[L, M, J, P, Y]` - **TARGET NODE Y FOUND!**

The first path found to the target node Y is `[L, M, J, P, Y]`.
The length of this path is 4 (L-M, M-J, J-P, P-Y).

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I, without removing E or I themselves.

First, I will attempt to find a cut set of size 1.
- If I remove M: Paths E-B-..., E-L-..., E-F-... still exist. Not a cut set.
- If I remove L: Paths E-M-..., E-B-..., E-F-... still exist. Not a cut set.
- If I remove F: Paths E-M-..., E-L-..., E-B-... still exist. Not a cut set.
- If I remove B: Paths E-M-..., E-L-..., E-F-... still exist. Not a cut set.
- If I remove P: Paths ...-C-I, ...-H-I still exist. Not a cut set.
- If I remove C: Paths ...-P-I, ...-H-I still exist. Not a cut set.
- If I remove H: Paths ...-P-I, ...-C-I still exist. Not a cut set.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is greater than 1.

Next, I will attempt to find a cut set of size 2.
Let's consider the nodes directly connected to E: {M, L, F, B}.
Let's consider the nodes directly connected to I: {P, C, H}.
Any path from E to I must pass through one of E's neighbors and one of I's neighbors.

Let's try removing two nodes that seem critical.
- If I remove B and P:
    - Paths through B are broken (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-H-I).
    - Paths through P are broken (e.g., E-M-J-P-I).
    - However, paths like E-L-F-X-K-G-H-I and E-F-X-K-G-H-I still exist, as they use H to reach I and do not involve B or P.
    - So, [B, P] is not a cut set.
- If I remove B and C:
    - Paths through B are broken.
    - Paths through C are broken.
    - However, paths like E-M-J-P-I (using P) and E-L-F-X-K-G-H-I (using H) still exist.
    - So, [B, C] is not a cut set.
- If I remove B and H:
    - Paths through B are broken.
    - Paths through H are broken.
    - However, paths like E-M-J-P-I (using P) still exist.
    - So, [B, H] is not a cut set.
- If I remove J and H:
    - Paths E-M-J-P-I and E-M-J-D-P-I are broken by removing J.
    - Paths E-L-F-X-K-G-H-I and E-F-X-K-G-H-I are broken by removing H.
    - However, paths like E-B-C-I and E-B-N-C-I (using C) still exist.
    - So, [J, H] is not a cut set.

It appears that a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient. To confirm this, I will look for node-disjoint paths between E and I. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths.

Let's identify node-disjoint paths (excluding E and I):
1.  **Path 1:** E - M - J - P - I
    Nodes in this path (excluding E, I): {M, J, P}
2.  **Path 2:** E - B - C - I
    Nodes in this path (excluding E, I): {B, C}
3.  **Path 3:** E - F - X - K - G - H - I
    Nodes in this path (excluding E, I): {F, X, K, G, H}

Let's check for node disjointness between these three paths:
- Path 1 nodes {M, J, P} are disjoint from Path 2 nodes {B, C}.
- Path 1 nodes {M, J, P} are disjoint from Path 3 nodes {F, X, K, G, H}.
- Path 2 nodes {B, C} are disjoint from Path 3 nodes {F, X, K, G, H}.

Since I have found three node-disjoint paths between E and I, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
Consider removing nodes that act as bottlenecks for these three "channels" or other paths.
Let's try the set [J, H, B].

1.  **Remove J:** This breaks Path 1 (E-M-J-P-I and E-M-J-D-P-I). It also breaks any other path that uses J (e.g., D is only connected to J and P, so D is also disconnected from E's side). All paths to P via J or D are now broken.
2.  **Remove H:** This breaks Path 3 (E-F-X-K-G-H-I and E-L-F-X-K-G-H-I). All paths to I via H are now broken.
3.  **Remove B:** This breaks Path 2 (E-B-C-I). It also breaks all other paths that go through B (e.g., E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I). Furthermore, B is the only connection from E's side to N. If B is removed, N, Y, and O become disconnected from E's side. This means all paths to C via N or O are broken, and all paths to P via O are broken.

Let's verify that no path remains after removing J, H, B:
- **Paths to P:**
    - E-M-J-P-I (Broken by J)
    - E-M-J-D-P-I (Broken by J)
    - Paths via O-P-I: O is connected to N, C, P. N is connected to B, C, Y. Since B is removed, N is isolated from E's side. Thus, O is also isolated from E's side. So, paths via O-P-I are broken.
    - All paths to P are broken.
- **Paths to C:**
    - E-B-C-I (Broken by B)
    - Paths via N-C-I: N is isolated from E's side by removing B. So, paths via N-C-I are broken.
    - Paths via O-C-I: O is isolated from E's side by removing B. So, paths via O-C-I are broken.
    - All paths to C are broken.
- **Paths to H:**
    - E-F-X-K-G-H-I (Broken by H)
    - E-L-F-X-K-G-H-I (Broken by H)
    - E-B-H-I (Broken by B and H)
    - All paths to H are broken.

Since all paths to P, C, and H (the neighbors of I) are broken, E and I are completely disconnected.
The cut set [J, H, B] has a size of 3. Since we established that the minimum cut size is at least 3, this is indeed the minimum.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [J, H, B]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N from P. I cannot remove N or P.

2.  **Explore Paths and Identify Bottlenecks:** I will systematically trace paths from N to P to understand the graph's connectivity. To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I can look for vertex-disjoint paths between N and P. The maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths will give a lower bound for the minimum cut set size (Menger's Theorem).

    Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths (paths that do not share any intermediate nodes):
    *   **Path 1:** N - Y - O - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: Y, O
    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: C, I
    *   **Path 3:** N - B - E - L - M - J - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: B, E, L, M, J

    Upon inspection, these three paths (N-Y-O-P, N-C-I-P, N-B-E-L-M-J-P) are indeed vertex-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes.
    *   Path 1 uses {Y, O}.
    *   Path 2 uses {C, I}.
    *   Path 3 uses {B, E, L, M, J}.
    Since these sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive, we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths.

3.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths between N and P, then at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect them. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to identify a set of 3 nodes whose removal will disconnect N and P.
    Let's consider the direct neighbors of P: I, O, J, D.
    *   Node D is only connected to J and P. No paths from N can reach D without passing through J. Therefore, D is not an essential intermediate node for paths from N to P.
    *   All paths from N to P must pass through at least one of P's other direct neighbors: I, O, or J.

    If I remove the nodes {I, O, J}:
    *   Removing I will break all paths that go N -> ... -> I -> P.
    *   Removing O will break all paths that go N -> ... -> O -> P.
    *   Removing J will break all paths that go N -> ... -> J -> P.

    Since all possible paths from N to P must pass through either I, O, or J, removing these three nodes will completely disconnect N from P.

5.  **Verify:**
    *   The cut set {I, O, J} has 3 nodes, which matches our minimum determined by Menger's Theorem.
    *   Each node in the cut set is necessary: if any one of I, O, or J were left, a path through that node to P would remain. For example, if I is not removed, N-C-I-P would still exist. If O is not removed, N-Y-O-P would still exist. If J is not removed, N-B-E-L-M-J-P would still exist.
    *   No smaller set of nodes (1 or 2) can disconnect N and P, as proven by the existence of 3 vertex-disjoint paths.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [I, O, J]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I.
2.  **Systematically identify all paths from G to I:**
    *   **Path Group 1 (Direct via H):**
        *   G - H - I
    *   **Path Group 2 (Via B):** All paths from G that go through B eventually reach I via C or P.
        *   G - B - C - I
        *   G - B - N - C - I
        *   G - B - N - Y - O - C - I
        *   G - B - N - Y - O - P - I
        *   Paths starting G - E - B - ... (e.g., G - E - B - C - I)
        *   Paths starting G - F - E - B - ... (e.g., G - F - E - B - C - I)
    *   **Path Group 3 (Via J):** All paths from G that go through J eventually reach I via P.
        *   G - E - L - M - J - P - I
        *   G - F - X - K - J - P - I
        *   G - F - K - J - P - I
        *   Paths starting G - E - F - X - K - J - P - I
        *   Paths starting G - E - F - K - J - P - I
        *   Paths starting G - F - E - L - M - J - P - I

3.  **Identify potential cut nodes:**
    *   To break Path Group 1, node H must be removed.
    *   To break Path Group 2, node B (or C, or N, or Y, or O, or P depending on the specific path) must be removed. However, B is a common node for all paths in this group originating from G's direct connection to B, or from G-E-B, or G-F-E-B.
    *   To break Path Group 3, node J (or P, or K, or X, or M, or L, or E, or F depending on the specific path) must be removed. However, J is a common node for all paths in this group that eventually reach I.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of nodes to remove:**
    *   **Can it be 1 node?**
        *   Removing H breaks G-H-I, but paths through B (e.g., G-B-C-I) and J (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-P-I) remain.
        *   Removing B breaks all paths in Path Group 2, but paths through H (G-H-I) and J (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-P-I) remain.
        *   Removing J breaks all paths in Path Group 3, but paths through H (G-H-I) and B (e.g., G-B-C-I) remain.
        *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.
    *   **Can it be 2 nodes?**
        *   Removing H and B: Paths through J still exist (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-P-I).
        *   Removing H and J: Paths through B still exist (e.g., G-B-C-I).
        *   Removing B and J: The path G-H-I still exists.
        *   Other combinations (e.g., C and P): G-H-I still exists, and paths through J (e.g., G-F-X-K-J-P-I) still exist.
        *   Therefore, two nodes are not sufficient.
    *   **Can it be 3 nodes?** Based on the analysis, all paths from G to I must pass through at least one of H, B, or J. If we remove these three nodes, all identified path groups will be severed.

5.  **Propose and verify the cut set [H, B, J]:**
    *   **Remove H:** This immediately breaks the path G - H - I.
    *   **Remove B:** This breaks all paths in Path Group 2 (e.g., G-B-C-I, G-E-B-N-Y-O-P-I, G-F-E-B-C-I).
    *   **Remove J:** This breaks all paths in Path Group 3 (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-P-I, G-F-X-K-J-P-I).
    *   After removing H, B, and J, let's check if any path remains:
        *   G's remaining direct neighbors are F and E.
        *   I's remaining direct neighbors are C and P (H is removed).
        *   From G-F: F is connected to E, X, K.
            *   F-E: E is connected to L. L is connected to M. M is connected to J (removed). No path to I.
            *   F-X: X is connected to K. K is connected to F, X. K is also connected to J (removed). No path to I.
            *   F-K: K is connected to X. K is also connected to J (removed). No path to I.
        *   From G-E: E is connected to F, L.
            *   E-F: (already covered above, no path to I).
            *   E-L: L is connected to M. M is connected to J (removed). No path to I.
    *   No paths remain between G and I after removing H, B, and J.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since 2 nodes are not enough, and we found a cut set of 3 nodes, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, N and G.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are N and G.

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to G:**
    *   (G, X)
    *   (G, K)
    *   (G, H)
    Removing these three edges would completely isolate G, thus disconnecting it from N. This gives an upper bound of 3 for the minimum cut.

3.  **Attempt to find 3 edge-disjoint paths:** If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and G, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least 3. If we cannot find 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut might be 2 or less.

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** N - B - E - F - K - G
        Edges: `{(N,B), (B,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G)}`

    *   **Path 2:** N - O - P - J - I - H - G
        Edges: `{(N,O), (O,P), (P,J), (J,I), (I,H), (H,G)}`

    *   **Check for disjointness between Path 1 and Path 2:**
        *   Starting edges (N,B) and (N,O) are distinct.
        *   Subsequent edges (B,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G) are distinct from (O,P), (P,J), (J,I), (I,H), (H,G).
        *   The paths do not share any common nodes other than N and G themselves, and thus no common edges.
        *   Therefore, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Attempt to find a Path 3 (edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2):**
        *   Path 3 must start with the remaining outgoing edge from N: (N,Y).
        *   N - Y ...
        *   From Y, the only connection is to O: N - Y - O ... (Edge (Y,O) is not in Path 1 or Path 2).
        *   From O, we cannot use (O,P) (it's in Path 2). So, we must use (O,B). (Edge (O,B) is not in Path 1 or Path 2).
        *   N - Y - O - B ...
        *   From B, we cannot use (B,E) (it's in Path 1). So, we must use (B,C). (Edge (B,C) is not in Path 1 or Path 2).
        *   N - Y - O - B - C ...
        *   From C, we can go to I or H.
            *   If C -> H: N - Y - O - B - C - H. From H, the only way to G is via (H,G). However, (H,G) is an edge in Path 2. Thus, this path is not edge-disjoint from Path 2.
            *   If C -> I: N - Y - O - B - C - I. From I, we can go to J, P, H.
                *   (I,H) is an edge in Path 2. So, we cannot use (I,H).
                *   (I,J) is not in Path 1 or Path 2. Let's try: N - Y - O - B - C - I - J.
                *   From J, we can go to D, P, M. (J,P) is in Path 2, (J,I) is in Path 2.
                *   If J -> D: N - Y - O - B - C - I - J - D. (J,D) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
                *   From D, we can go to P: N - Y - O - B - C - I - J - D - P. (D,P) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
                *   From P, we can go to O, D, J, I, Y. (P,O) is in Path 2, (P,J) is in Path 2. (P,I) and (P,Y) are available, but lead to loops (P-I-C-B-O-Y-N or P-Y-O-B-C-I-J-D-P). This path does not reach G without using edges already in Path 1 or Path 2, or without looping.
                *   If J -> M: N - Y - O - B - C - I - J - M. (J,M) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
                *   From M, we can go to L: N - Y - O - B - C - I - J - M - L. (M,L) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
                *   From L, we can go to E: N - Y - O - B - C - I - J - M - L - E. (L,E) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
                *   From E, we can go to B (back), L (back), F. (E,B) is in Path 1, (E,F) is in Path 1. This path is blocked.

    *   **Conclusion on edge-disjoint paths:** It appears there are only two edge-disjoint paths (Path 1 and Path 2). This implies that the minimum edge cut is 2.

4.  **Find a cut set of 2 edges:** Based on the analysis of paths, we need to find two edges whose removal breaks all paths.
    *   Consider the edges that lead into G's immediate neighborhood: (G,X), (G,K), (G,H).
    *   All paths to G must eventually pass through one of these three edges.
    *   Let's try to cut paths to X and K with one edge, and paths to H with another.
    *   Notice that all paths to K and X from N must pass through F (e.g., N-B-E-F-K-G, N-B-E-F-X-G, N-O-P-J-M-L-E-F-K-G, etc.). The edge (E,F) is a critical link to F. If (E,F) is removed, all paths to F (and thus to K and X) are broken.
    *   The remaining paths to G must go through H. All paths to H must eventually use the edge (H,G) to reach G.

    *   **Proposed Cut Set:** `[(E,F), (H,G)]`

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Removal of (E,F):**
        *   Any path from N to G that goes through K (e.g., N-...-F-K-G) is broken because (E,F) is the only way to reach F from the N side (all paths to F must pass through E, and then (E,F)).
        *   Any path from N to G that goes through X (e.g., N-...-F-X-G) is broken for the same reason.
        *   Therefore, removing (E,F) disconnects all paths to G via K or X.

    *   **Removal of (H,G):**
        *   Any path from N to G that goes through H (e.g., N-...-H-G) is broken because (H,G) is the direct connection from H to G.
        *   Therefore, removing (H,G) disconnects all paths to G via H.

    *   Since all paths to G must go through X, K, or H, and both (E,F) and (H,G) effectively cut off all these routes, the two black nodes N and G are completely disconnected.

6.  **Final Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 2, and one such cut set is `[(E,F), (H,G)]`.

The final answer is structured as requested.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect H from B. I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of H and B:
Neighbors of H: G, F, B, I, E
Neighbors of B: H, E, I, C, N

Now, I will systematically find edge-disjoint paths between H and B. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edge.

1.  **Path 1: H - B**
    Edges: `{(H, B)}`

2.  **Path 2: H - E - B**
    Edges: `{(H, E), (E, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 as it uses different edges.

3.  **Path 3: H - I - B**
    Edges: `{(H, I), (I, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
    -   (H, I) is not (H, B) or (H, E) or (E, B).
    -   (I, B) is not (H, B) or (H, E) or (E, B).

4.  **Path 4: H - F - E - I - C - B**
    Edges: `{(H, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, C), (C, B)}`
    Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from the previous three:
    -   (H, F) is not in `{(H, B), (H, E), (E, B), (H, I), (I, B)}`.
    -   (F, E) is not in `{(H, B), (H, E), (E, B), (H, I), (I, B)}`.
    -   (E, I) is not in `{(H, B), (H, E), (E, B), (H, I), (I, B)}`.
    -   (I, C) is not in `{(H, B), (H, E), (E, B), (H, I), (I, B)}`.
    -   (C, B) is not in `{(H, B), (H, E), (E, B), (H, I), (I, B)}`.
    All edges in Path 4 are distinct from the edges in Paths 1, 2, and 3.

I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 4.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that actually disconnects H and B.
Consider the edges that are "entry points" to B from H's side of the graph, or "exit points" from H to B's side.
The edges that connect H to B or to nodes that lead to B are:
-   (H, B) - Direct connection.
-   (H, E) - H connects to E, and E connects to B.
-   (H, I) - H connects to I, and I connects to B.
-   (F, E) - H connects to F, F connects to E, and E connects to B. (This edge (F,E) is crucial for paths like H-F-E-B).

Let's propose the following cut set: `[(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)]`.
Let's verify if removing these 4 edges disconnects H and B:
1.  Path H - B is broken by removing (H, B).
2.  Path H - E - B is broken by removing (H, E).
3.  Path H - I - B is broken by removing (H, I).
4.  Any path from H that goes through F (e.g., H-F-E-B or H-F-E-I-C-B) is broken by removing (F, E).
    -   After removing (H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E):
        -   H is connected to G, F.
        -   B is connected to C, N.
        -   E is connected to L, M, I (but not to H or F).
        -   I is connected to C, J, P, E (but not to H).

    -   Therefore, H cannot reach B.

Since I found 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 4. I also found a cut set of 4 edges that successfully disconnects H and B. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

    *   **Paths starting with E -> M:** Any path from E that starts with E-M must then go M-F... (e.g., E-M-F-K-J). Therefore, all paths starting E-M must pass through node F.
    *   **Paths starting with E -> F:** Any path from E that starts with E-F directly passes through node F (e.g., E-F-K-J).
    *   **Paths starting with E -> B:** Any path from E that starts with E-B directly passes through node B (e.g., E-B-H-I-J, E-B-N-C-I-J, E-B-C-O-P-J).
4.  **Identify critical nodes:**

    *   This means that F and B are "bottleneck" nodes. All paths from E to J must necessarily traverse either F or B.

    *   If we remove only F, paths through B still exist (e.g., E-B-H-I-J). So, F alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove only B, paths through F still exist (e.g., E-F-K-J). So, B alone is not enough.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is insufficient to disconnect E and J.
6.  **Test for a two-node cut:**
    *   If we remove both F and B:
        *   All paths that previously went through F (E-L-M-F-..., E-M-F-..., E-F-...) are now broken.
        *   All paths that previously went through B (E-B-H-..., E-B-N-..., E-B-C-...) are now broken.
        *   Since every path from E to J must pass through either F or B, removing both F and B will break all possible paths between E and J.
7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

        *   J - P - O - N - B - E
        *   J - P - O - Y - N - B - E
        *   J - P - O - C - B - E
        All these paths converge at O and then pass through B to reach E.

    *   **Paths through I (leading to H):**
        *   J - I - H - C - B - E (This path also passes through B)
        *   J - I - H - X - K - F - E
        *   J - I - H - G - X - K - F - E
        *   J - I - H - G - K - F - E
        These paths from I split, some going through B and others through F.

3.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    To disconnect J and E, every path between them must be broken.
    Let's consider the immediate neighbors of E that are reachable from J: M, B, F. Any path from J to E must pass through one of these three nodes.

    *   If we remove node **M**, the path J-M-E is broken.
    *   If we remove node **B**, all paths that use B to reach E are broken. This includes all paths from J through D/P/O/N/Y/C to B-E, and the path J-I-H-C-B-E.
    *   If we remove node **F**, all paths that use F to reach E are broken. This includes paths like J-I-H-X-K-F-E, J-I-H-G-X-K-F-E, and J-I-H-G-K-F-E.

    Therefore, if we remove the set of nodes {M, B, F}, all possible paths from J to E will be severed, as these three nodes represent all the "entry points" to E from J's side of the graph. This set has a size of 3.

4.  **Verify if a smaller cut set is possible (e.g., size 1 or 2):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that there are at least 3 node-disjoint paths between J and E (excluding J and E themselves).

    Let's identify three node-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** J - M - E
        Nodes (excluding J, E): {M}
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** J - D - P - O - N - B - E
        Nodes (excluding J, E): {D, P, O, N, B}
    *   **Path 3 (P3):** J - I - H - X - K - F - E
        Nodes (excluding J, E): {I, H, X, K, F}

    Let's check for node-disjointness between the intermediate nodes of these paths:
    *   {M} is disjoint from {D, P, O, N, B}. (True)
    *   {M} is disjoint from {I, H, X, K, F}. (True)
    *   {D, P, O, N, B} is disjoint from {I, H, X, K, F}. (True)

    Since I have found three node-disjoint paths between J and E, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect J and E is at least 3.

    Since I found a cut set of size 3 ({M, B, F}) and proved that at least 3 nodes are required, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, B, F]

    *   Add `(M, [Y, O, M])` to queue. Mark M as visited.

2.  **Search for node-disjoint paths:** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (node-cut set) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the two target nodes. I will try to find as many node-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I - C - N

    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - B - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, E, B}
        *   Check for disjointness with Path 1: The set {H, I, C} and {F, E, B} are mutually disjoint.
        *   Check for disjointness with Path 2: The set {K, J, D, P, O} and {F, E, B} are mutually disjoint.
        *   This path is node-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

    I have successfully identified three node-disjoint paths between G and N. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect G and N must be at least 3.

    *   From Path 2 (G - K - J - D - P - O - N), I can choose J.
    *   From Path 3 (G - F - E - B - N), I can choose E.

1.  **Identify all direct connections for M and N:**
    *   Node M is connected to: L, E, J.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

2.  **Explore paths from M to N:**
    I will systematically list paths to understand the network structure between M and N.

    *   **Paths starting M -> E:**
        *   M - E - H - B - N
        *   M - E - H - C - N
        *   M - E - G - F - H - B - N (longer, but still uses E-H)
        *   M - E - G - F - H - C - N (longer, but still uses E-H)

    *   **Paths starting M -> L:**
        *   M - L - E - H - B - N (uses L-E-H)
        *   M - L - E - H - C - N (uses L-E-H)
        *   M - L - E - G - F - H - B - N (uses L-E-G-F-H)
        *   M - L - E - G - F - H - C - N (uses L-E-G-F-H)

    *   **Paths starting M -> J:**
        *   M - J - I - C - N
        *   M - J - I - H - B - N
        *   M - J - I - H - C - N
        *   M - J - P - O - N
        *   M - J - D - P - O - N

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    To find the minimum edge cut, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between M and N. If I can find `k` edge-disjoint paths, then at least `k` edges must be removed.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1 (P1): M - E - H - B - N**
        Edges: {(M, E), (E, H), (H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): M - J - P - O - N**
        Edges: {(M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

    *   **Path 3 (P3): M - L - E - G - F - H - C - N**
        Edges: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, G), (G, F), (F, H), (H, C), (C, N)}

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges connected to M: (M, E) in P1, (M, J) in P2, (M, L) in P3. (Distinct)
    *   Edges connected to the next node: (E, H) in P1, (J, P) in P2, (L, E) in P3. (Distinct)
    *   Edges connected to the next node: (H, B) in P1, (P, O) in P2, (E, G) in P3. (Distinct)
    *   Edges connected to the next node: (B, N) in P1, (O, N) in P2, (G, F) in P3. (Distinct)
    *   Remaining edges in P3: (F, H), (H, C), (C, N). These are distinct from any edges in P1 or P2.

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect M and N must be at least 3.

4.  **Identify a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects M and N. Based on the paths identified, I can choose edges that act as "bottlenecks" for different groups of paths.

    Consider the following cut set:
    *   **(E, H):** This edge cuts all paths from M that go through E (including those from L via E) and then proceed to H. This includes paths like M-E-H-B-N, M-E-H-C-N, M-L-E-H-B-N, M-L-E-H-C-N, and any longer paths that use E-H.
    *   **(J, I):** This edge cuts all paths from M that go through J and then proceed to I. This includes paths like M-J-I-C-N, M-J-I-H-B-N, M-J-I-H-C-N.
    *   **(J, P):** This edge cuts all paths from M that go through J and then proceed to P. This includes paths like M-J-P-O-N and M-J-D-P-O-N (since D is only connected to J and P, removing (J,P) isolates P from J even if D is used).

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   After removing (E, H), no path from M can reach H via E or L-E.
    *   After removing (J, I), no path from M can reach I via J.
    *   After removing (J, P), no path from M can reach P via J (or J-D).

    Since all paths from M must pass through E, L, or J, and we have cut off all routes from E (via H), all routes from J (via I and P), and all routes from L (which must go through E), there are no remaining paths from M to N.

    This cut set of 3 edges successfully disconnects M and N. Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this set is minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, H), (J, I), (J, P)]

    *   H - B - N (This path uses the edge (B, N))

    *   H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N (This path uses the edge (O, N))

*   (N, C) - This edge is part of Path 2.
*   (N, O) - This edge is part of Paths 3, 4, and 5.

1.  Removing (N, B) breaks the path H - B - N.
2.  Removing (N, C) breaks the path H - I - C - N.

*   If I remove only two edges, for example, (N, B) and (N, C), then the paths ending in (N, O) (e.g., H - I - P - O - N) would still exist.
*   If I remove only two edges, for example, (N, B) and (N, O), then the path H - I - C - N would still exist.
*   If I remove only two edges, for example, (N, C) and (N, O), then the path H - B - N would still exist.

Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path intact, all three edges are necessary to disconnect H and N. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The two black nodes are E and D. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from D. I cannot remove E or D.

I will systematically explore all paths from E to D and identify critical nodes.

First, let's identify the immediate neighbors of E: M, L, F, B.
And the immediate neighbors of D: J, P.

All paths from E to D must pass through one of E's neighbors, and eventually through one of D's neighbors.

Let's categorize the paths based on the first step from E:

**Category 1: Paths starting E -> M, E -> L, or E -> F.**
*   If a path starts E -> M, it can go M -> K.
*   If a path starts E -> L, it can go L -> M -> K.
*   If a path starts E -> F, it can go F -> K or F -> X or F -> G.

Observing the subgraph containing M, L, F, K, X, G:
*   All paths from E that go through M, L, F, K, or X eventually converge at node G to proceed towards the right side of the graph.
    *   E -> M -> K -> X -> G
    *   E -> M -> K -> F -> G
    *   E -> L -> M -> K -> X -> G
    *   E -> L -> M -> K -> F -> G
    *   E -> F -> X -> G
    *   E -> F -> G
    *   E -> F -> K -> X -> G
    Therefore, node G acts as a single point of entry from this "left" cluster (M, L, F, K, X) to the rest of the graph leading to D. If G is removed, all paths from E that go through M, L, F, K, or X are blocked.

**Category 2: Paths starting E -> B.**
*   From B, paths can go to H or C.
    *   **Subcategory 2a: Paths E -> B -> H.**
        *   H connects to I and G. (G is in Category 1's bottleneck, so we consider H -> I here).
        *   H -> I -> J -> D
        *   H -> I -> P -> D
    *   **Subcategory 2b: Paths E -> B -> C.**
        *   C connects to I, N, O.
        *   C -> I -> J -> D
        *   C -> I -> P -> D
        *   C -> N -> O -> P -> D
        *   C -> N -> O -> Y -> J -> D
        *   C -> N -> O -> Y -> P -> D
        *   C -> O -> P -> D
        *   C -> O -> Y -> J -> D
        *   C -> O -> Y -> P -> D

From this analysis, it's clear that all paths from E to D must pass through either node G (for paths in Category 1) or node B (for paths in Category 2). These two sets of paths are distinct at this point; a path cannot go E -> G -> ... and then also pass through B, nor can it go E -> B -> ... and then pass through G to reach D.

Therefore, to disconnect E from D, we must remove both G and B.

Let's verify if a single node removal is sufficient:
*   If G is removed: Paths like E -> B -> H -> I -> J -> D still exist.
*   If B is removed: Paths like E -> F -> G -> H -> I -> J -> D still exist.
*   If H is removed: Paths like E -> B -> C -> I -> J -> D still exist.
*   If C is removed: Paths like E -> B -> H -> I -> J -> D still exist.
*   If I is removed: Paths like E -> B -> C -> N -> O -> P -> D still exist.
*   If J is removed: Paths to D via P still exist (e.g., E -> B -> H -> I -> P -> D).
*   If P is removed: Paths to D via J still exist (e.g., E -> B -> H -> I -> J -> D).
No single node removal can disconnect E and D.

Since a single node is not sufficient, and we have found a set of two nodes ([G, B]) that disconnects E and D, this must be the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [G, B]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'P']}

    *   **Paths starting with G-F:**
        *   G-F-E-I (direct path to I's neighborhood)
        *   G-F-E-H-I
        *   G-F-E-B-I
        *   G-F-E-B-C-I
        *   G-F-E-B-N-C-I
        *   G-F-E-B-N-O-C-I
        *   G-F-E-B-N-O-P-I
        *   G-F-E-L-M-J-I
        *   G-F-E-L-M-J-P-I
        *   ... (and other longer paths through E, H, B, C, N, O, P, J, M, L)
        All these paths from G via F must pass through the edge (F, E) to reach any node connected to I (E, H, B, C, J, P). If we remove (F, E), all paths starting with G-F are broken.

    *   **Paths starting with G-K:**
        *   From K, the only connections are to X and F.
        *   If G-K-F-..., then this path must use (F, E) to reach I's side.
        *   If G-K-X-F-..., then this path must also use (F, E) to reach I's side.
        Therefore, all paths starting with G-K will eventually pass through (F, E) to reach I.

    *   **Paths starting with G-X:**
        *   From X, the only connections are to K and F.
        *   If G-X-F-..., then this path must use (F, E) to reach I's side.
        *   If G-X-K-F-..., then this path must also use (F, E) to reach I's side.
        Therefore, all paths starting with G-X will eventually pass through (F, E) to reach I.

3.  **Identify a minimal cut set:**
    From the analysis above, it is clear that any path from G to I must either:
    *   Use the edge (G, H) to enter the part of the graph connected to I via H.
    *   Use the edge (F, E) to enter the part of the graph connected to I via E (after passing through F, K, or X from G).

    Therefore, removing both edges (G, H) and (F, E) will break all possible paths between G and I.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (G, H), then paths like G-F-E-I would still exist.
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (F, E), then paths like G-H-I would still exist.
    Since removing a single edge is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2. Our identified cut set has 2 edges, so it is minimal.

    *   P3: {(G, F), (F, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from H. This is a min-cut problem, and by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** N (black) and H (black).

2.  **Explore possible paths between N and H and identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    I will try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1:** N - B - H
        Edges: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`

    *   **Path 2:** N - O - P - I - H
        Edges: `(N, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`

    *   **Path 3:** N - Y - O - C - B - E - F - X - G - H
        Edges: `(N, Y)`, `(Y, O)`, `(O, C)`, `(C, B)`, `(B, E)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, X)`, `(X, G)`, `(G, H)`

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    Let's list all edges used in these three paths and check for overlaps:
    *   Path 1 edges: `{(N, B), (B, H)}`
    *   Path 2 edges: `{(N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)}`
    *   Path 3 edges: `{(N, Y), (Y, O), (O, C), (C, B), (B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)}`

    Upon inspection, all edges listed for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are unique to their respective paths. There are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect N from H.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges connected to it.
    *   Node N is connected to Y, O, and B. Removing `(N, Y)`, `(N, O)`, and `(N, B)` would isolate N, thus disconnecting it from H. This is a cut set of size 3.
    *   Node H is connected to B, G, and I. Removing `(B, H)`, `(G, H)`, and `(I, H)` would isolate H, thus disconnecting it from N. This is also a cut set of size 3.

    Both options provide a minimal cut set of size 3. I will choose the set of edges directly connected to H.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and B.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and B:**
    *   **Direct Path:** H - B (Length 1)
    *   **Paths via G:**
        *   H - G - F - E - B (Length 4)
        *   H - G - X - F - E - B (Length 5)
    *   **Paths via I:**
        *   H - I - C - B (Length 3)
        *   H - I - P - O - C - B (Length 5)
        *   H - I - P - O - N - B (Length 5)

2.  **Strategy for finding a minimal cut set:** I will try to identify "bottleneck" edges that, when removed, sever multiple paths. A common strategy is to consider removing the direct connection first, then addressing the remaining indirect paths.

3.  **Consider removing the direct edge (H, B):**
    *   If I remove the edge (H, B), the direct path is broken.
    *   Now, I need to break all remaining paths from H to B. These paths must go through H's other neighbors: G or I.

4.  **Address paths starting H - G:**
    *   The paths are H - G - F - E - B and H - G - X - F - E - B.
    *   Both of these paths converge at node E and then connect to B via the edge (E, B).
    *   Therefore, removing the edge (E, B) will disconnect all paths originating from H through G to B.

5.  **Address paths starting H - I:**
    *   The paths are H - I - C - B, H - I - P - O - C - B, and H - I - P - O - N - B.
    *   All these paths begin with the edge (H, I).
    *   Therefore, removing the edge (H, I) will disconnect all paths originating from H through I to B.

6.  **Formulate a candidate cut set:**
    *   Based on the above, the set of edges [(H, B), (E, B), (H, I)] should disconnect H from B.
    *   Let's verify:
        *   (H, B) cuts the direct path.
        *   (E, B) cuts all paths from H through G to B.
        *   (H, I) cuts all paths from H through I to B.
    *   This set successfully breaks all identified paths between H and B. The size of this cut set is 3.

7.  **Check for minimality (can it be done with fewer than 3 edges?):**
    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect H and B?** No, because there are multiple independent paths (e.g., H-B, H-G-..., H-I-...). Removing any single edge would leave other paths intact.
    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect H and B?**
        *   **Case A: One of the edges is (H, B).** If (H, B) is removed, we still need to cut paths through G and paths through I. Paths through G rely on (E, B) to reach B. Paths through I rely on (C, B) or (N, B) to reach B. A single additional edge cannot cut both the (E, B) connection and the (C, B)/(N, B) connections simultaneously. For example, if we remove (H, B) and (E, B), paths like H-I-C-B and H-I-P-O-N-B would still exist. Thus, 2 edges are not enough if (H, B) is one of them.
        *   **Case B: Neither of the edges is (H, B).** If (H, B) is not removed, the direct path H-B remains, and H and B are still connected. Thus, 2 edges are not enough if (H, B) is not included.
    *   Since neither case allows for disconnection with 2 edges, the minimum number of edges must be 3.

8.  **Alternative Cut Set of Size 3:** Another valid cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to H: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]. This also isolates H from the rest of the graph, including B.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (E, B), (H, I)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, H and N.

First, I identify the two target nodes: H and N.

Next, I list all direct connections (neighbors) for each target node:
*   Neighbors of H: G, I, B
*   Neighbors of N: B, C, Y

To disconnect H from N, all paths between them must be broken. I will systematically explore possible paths and identify potential cut sets.

**Strategy 1: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
1.  **Isolate N:** To completely disconnect N from the rest of the graph, all edges connected to N must be removed. These edges are (N, B), (N, C), and (N, Y).
    *   If these 3 edges are removed, N becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist between H and N.
    *   This is a cut set of size 3: `[(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]`.

2.  **Isolate H:** Similarly, to completely disconnect H from the rest of the graph, all edges connected to H must be removed. These edges are (H, G), (H, I), and (H, B).
    *   If these 3 edges are removed, H becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist between H and N.
    *   This is a cut set of size 3: `[(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]`.

Since I have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3. Now, I need to verify if a smaller cut (e.g., of size 1 or 2) is possible.

**Strategy 2: Check for a cut set of size 1.**
It's clear that removing a single edge will not disconnect H and N, as there are multiple independent paths between them. For example, if (H, B) is removed, paths like H-G-B-N or H-I-P-O-C-N still exist.

**Strategy 3: Check for a cut set of size 2.**
I will systematically consider combinations of two edges and see if their removal disconnects H and N.

Let's list the main "entry points" to N: B, C, Y.
Let's list the main "exit points" from H: G, I, B.

Consider removing any two edges directly connected to N:
*   **Remove (N, B) and (N, C):**
    *   Paths through B and C to N are broken.
    *   However, paths to N via Y still exist. For example, H-I-P-O-Y-N is still a valid path.
    *   Therefore, this cut set is not sufficient.
*   **Remove (N, B) and (N, Y):**
    *   Paths through B and Y to N are broken.
    *   However, paths to N via C still exist. For example, H-I-P-O-C-N is still a valid path.
    *   Therefore, this cut set is not sufficient.
*   **Remove (N, C) and (N, Y):**
    *   Paths through C and Y to N are broken.
    *   However, paths to N via B still exist. For example, H-B-N is still a valid path.
    *   Therefore, this cut set is not sufficient.

Since removing any two edges directly connected to N is not sufficient, and similarly for H (by symmetry), I need to consider combinations of edges that are not necessarily directly connected to the target nodes.

Let's consider the major "branches" from H to N:
1.  **Branch via B:** H-B-N
2.  **Branch via G:** H-G-...-N (e.g., H-G-B-N, H-G-E-B-N, H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O-C-N, H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O-Y-N)
3.  **Branch via I:** H-I-...-N (e.g., H-I-P-O-C-N, H-I-P-O-Y-N, H-I-P-J-M-L-E-B-N, H-I-P-J-M-L-E-G-B-N)

Let's try to find two edges that cut all these branches.
*   **Consider (B, N) and (P, O):**
    *   (B, N) cuts all paths that go directly to N via B (e.g., H-B-N, H-G-B-N, H-G-E-B-N, H-I-P-J-M-L-E-B-N, H-I-P-J-M-L-E-G-B-N).
    *   (P, O) cuts paths that go via P-O to C or Y (e.g., H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O-C-N, H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O-Y-N, H-I-P-O-C-N, H-I-P-O-Y-N).
    *   If both (B, N) and (P, O) are removed, are there any remaining paths?
        *   No, because all paths from H to N must either pass through B (which is cut by (B, N)) or pass through P-O (which is cut by (P, O)) to reach C or Y.
        *   Let's double check:
            *   Paths ending in B-N are cut by (B, N).
            *   Paths ending in C-N or Y-N must pass through O (O is connected to C and Y). All paths to O from H pass through P (H-I-P-O or H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O). So, cutting (P, O) would cut all paths to C and Y.
        *   Therefore, removing (B, N) and (P, O) disconnects H and N. This is a cut set of size 2.

Let's verify this cut set: `[(B, N), (P, O)]`.
1.  **Remove (B, N):** This breaks all paths that use the edge (B, N).
    *   H-B-N
    *   H-G-B-N
    *   H-G-E-B-N
    *   H-I-P-J-M-L-E-B-N
    *   H-I-P-J-M-L-E-G-B-N
    *   Remaining paths must go through C or Y to reach N.
2.  **Remove (P, O):** This breaks all paths that use the edge (P, O).
    *   H-I-P-O-C-N
    *   H-I-P-O-Y-N
    *   H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O-C-N
    *   H-G-E-L-M-J-P-O-Y-N
    *   All paths from H to C or Y must pass through P-O. (P is connected to I and J. J is connected to M. M is connected to L. L is connected to E. E is connected to G. G is connected to H. So all paths from H to P go through I or G-E-L-M-J. All paths from P to C or Y go through O).
    *   Therefore, removing (P, O) breaks all paths to C and Y.

Step-by-step reasoning.

1. O - C - H

This path must be broken. Therefore, C is a strong candidate for inclusion in the cut set. If C is not removed, this path remains, and O and H are still connected. So, C must be removed.

Now, let's consider all paths that do NOT use C. These paths must go from {P, Y, N, M} to {I, X, G}.

Let's trace these remaining paths:

**Paths that eventually reach H via I:**
To reach I, a path must go through J (since I is connected to J and C, and C is assumed removed).
- O - P - D - J - I - H
- O - M - J - I - H
Both of these paths rely on node J. If J is removed, these paths are broken.

**Paths that eventually reach H via X or G:**
To reach X or G, a path must go through F or K (since X is connected to K, F and H; G is connected to K, F and H).
Let's trace paths from O's remaining neighbors to F or K:
- From O via Y: O - Y - N - B - E - F - {X, G} - H
- From O via N: O - N - B - E - F - {X, G} - H
- From O via M: O - M - L - E - F - {X, G} - H

All these paths (to X or G) converge through node E and then node F.
Specifically, they all pass through the sequence E - F.
Therefore, removing either E or F would break all these paths.

Summary of path categories and required cut nodes:
1.  **Path O - C - H:** Requires removing node C.
2.  **Paths O - {P, M} - J - I - H:** Requires removing node J.
3.  **Paths O - {Y, N, M} - {B, L} - E - F - {X, G} - H:** Requires removing node E (or F).

To disconnect O and H, we must break all three categories of paths.
- Removing C breaks the first category.
- Removing J breaks the second category.
- Removing E (or F) breaks the third category.

Therefore, a minimum cut set must include C, J, and either E or F. This means a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed.

Let's verify this:
If we remove the set {C, J, E}:
- The path O - C - H is broken by removing C.
- The paths O - P - D - J - I - H and O - M - J - I - H are broken by removing J.
- The paths O - Y - N - B - E - F - {X, G} - H, O - N - B - E - F - {X, G} - H, and O - M - L - E - F - {X, G} - H are broken by removing E.
All paths are successfully broken.

No smaller set of nodes can achieve this disconnection:
- If C is not removed, O-C-H remains.
- If J is not removed (and C is removed), paths through J-I-H remain.
- If E (or F) is not removed (and C and J are removed), paths through E-F-{X,G}-H remain.

Thus, a set of 3 nodes is the minimum required.

    *   There is a direct edge between B and H: (B, H). This is one path.

2.  **Explore alternative paths:**
    *   To find the minimum cut, I will look for edge-disjoint paths between B and H. The maximum number of edge-disjoint paths equals the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect the nodes (Menger's Theorem).

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths:

    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        Edges: {(B, H)}

    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H
        Edges: {(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as none of its edges are (B, H).

    *   **Path 3:** B - E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no (B, H) edge).
        This path is also edge-disjoint from Path 2:
        - (B, E) is not (B, C)
        - (E, F) is not (C, I)
        - (F, G) is not (I, H)
        - (G, H) is not (I, H)

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    *   Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    *   Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects B and H. A common strategy is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    *   Node H has three incident edges: (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
    *   If I remove all these edges:
        *   (H, B) breaks Path 1.
        *   (H, G) breaks Path 3.
        *   (H, I) breaks Path 2.
    *   By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from node B.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    *   I have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths, meaning the minimum cut is at least 3.
    *   I have found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects B and H.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Edges: (M, J), (J, I), (I, H), (H, B)

Let's verify if these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
*   Edges in Path 1: (M, E), (E, B)
*   Edges in Path 2: (M, F), (F, G), (G, B)
*   Edges in Path 3: (M, J), (J, I), (I, H), (H, B)

Comparing the edge sets, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.
Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect M and B must be at least 3.

Now, I will attempt to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects M from B. I will consider the "entry points" to B and the "exit points" from M.

The direct connections from M are to L, E, F, and J.
The direct connections to B are from E, G, H, C, and N.

Let's consider cutting the following three edges:
1.  **(E, B)**: This edge is part of Path 1 (M-E-B). It also cuts other paths like M-L-E-B and M-F-E-B.
2.  **(G, B)**: This edge is part of Path 2 (M-F-G-B). It also cuts other paths like M-E-G-B, M-L-E-G-B, M-F-E-G-B, M-F-X-K-G-B, and M-F-K-G-B.
3.  **(M, J)**: This edge is part of Path 3 (M-J-I-H-B). It also cuts all other paths originating from M through J, such as M-J-I-C-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, and M-J-P-O-N-B.

Let's verify that removing these three edges `[(E, B), (G, B), (M, J)]` completely disconnects M from B:

*   **Paths starting M-J...**: All paths that start with M-J are broken by the removal of (M, J). This includes M-J-I-H-B, M-J-I-C-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, and M-J-P-O-N-B.

    *   M-E-B is broken by (E, B).
    *   M-E-G-B is broken by (G, B).
    *   Any other path from M through E must eventually pass through E or G to reach B. Since (E,B) and (G,B) are removed, no path from M through E can reach B.
*   **Paths starting M-F...**:
    *   M-F-E-B is broken by (E, B).
    *   M-F-G-B is broken by (G, B).
    *   M-F-X-K-G-B is broken by (G, B).
    *   M-F-K-G-B is broken by (G, B).
    *   Any other path from M through F must eventually pass through E or G to reach B. Since (E,B) and (G,B) are removed, no path from M through F can reach B.
*   **Paths starting M-L...**:

    *   M-L-E-G-B is broken by (G, B).
    *   Any other path from M through L must eventually pass through E or G to reach B. Since (E,B) and (G,B) are removed, no path from M through L can reach B.

Since all paths from M to B are broken by the removal of these three edges, and we have established that at least 3 edges are required, this set of 3 edges is the minimum.

2.  **Analyze connections from G:**
    *   Node G is directly connected to H, F, X, and K.
    *   Nodes X and K form a small sub-graph with G and F:
        *   X is connected to G, K, F.
        *   K is connected to G, X, F.
    *   This means any path starting from G that goes through X or K will eventually pass through F to reach any other part of the graph (e.g., G-X-F-E... or G-K-F-E...).
    *   Therefore, all paths from G to the rest of the graph (where N is located) must pass through either node H or node F.
3.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   **Attempt a single-node cut:**
        *   If we remove H: Paths from G to N can still go through F (e.g., G-F-E-I-C-N or G-F-E-M-J-P-N). So, removing H alone is not enough.
        *   If we remove F: Paths from G to N can still go through H (e.g., G-H-B-N or G-H-I-C-N). So, removing F alone is not enough.
        *   Since all paths from G must pass through either H or F, removing any other single node (like B, C, I, E, P, etc.) would still leave paths through both H and F. For example, removing I would still leave G-H-B-N and G-F-E-M-J-P-N. Thus, a single node removal is insufficient.
    *   **Attempt a two-node cut:**
        *   Based on the observation that all paths from G must pass through either H or F, removing both H and F should disconnect G from the rest of the graph (and thus from N).
        *   Let's verify: If H and F are removed, G is only connected to X and K. X is connected to K. K is connected to X. There are no other connections from G, X, or K to any other nodes in the graph. Therefore, G is completely disconnected from N.
4.  **Conclusion:** A single node removal is not sufficient, but removing two nodes (H and F) successfully disconnects G and N. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Identify immediate neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of L: E, M
    *   Neighbors of J: K, I, P, D (Note: D is only connected to P, so any path to J via D must pass through P).
3.  **Trace all possible paths from L to J:**
    All paths from L must start with either E or M.
    All paths to J must end with K, I, or P.

    Let's list some representative paths:
    *   **Paths through M:**
        *   P1: L - M - K - J
        *   P2: L - M - K - X - G - H - I - J
        *   P3: L - M - K - X - G - F - E - B - C - I - J (and other variations through E)
    *   **Paths through E:**
        *   P4: L - E - F - K - J
        *   P5: L - E - F - G - X - K - J
        *   P6: L - E - F - G - H - I - J
        *   P7: L - E - B - H - I - J
        *   P8: L - E - B - H - G - X - K - J
        *   P9: L - E - B - H - G - F - K - J
        *   P10: L - E - B - C - I - J
        *   P11: L - E - B - C - O - P - J

4.  **Determine the maximum number of node-disjoint paths:**
    A set of node-disjoint paths shares no intermediate nodes (only the start and end nodes, L and J, can be shared).
    *   Path A: L - M - K - J (Intermediate nodes: M, K)
    *   Path B: L - E - F - G - H - I - J (Intermediate nodes: E, F, G, H, I)
    These two paths are node-disjoint.

    Can we find a third node-disjoint path?
    Any path from L must start with E or M.
    *   Path A uses M.
    *   Path B uses E.
    Therefore, any third path would have to reuse either M (which only connects to K, already used by Path A) or E (already used by Path B). This means there are at most 2 node-disjoint paths between L and J.
    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Thus, the minimum cut set size is 2.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 2:**
    We need to find two nodes (neither L nor J) whose removal disconnects all paths.
    Let's consider removing node E and node K.
    *   **If E is removed:** All paths starting with L -> E are broken (P4, P5, P6, P7, P8, P9, P10, P11, and any path from M that eventually goes through E like P3). The only remaining way for L to reach J is through M. The only direct path from M to J is L - M - K - J (P1).
    *   **If K is also removed:** The path L - M - K - J (P1) is broken because K is removed.

    Since removing E blocks all paths through the 'E' branch, and removing K blocks the only remaining path through the 'M' branch (L-M-K-J), no path exists between L and J after removing E and K.

6.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   Nodes E and K are not the target nodes (L or J).
    *   Removing E breaks all paths that start L -> E.
    *   The only remaining path from L is L -> M -> K -> J.
    *   Removing K breaks this last remaining path.
    *   Therefore, L and J are completely disconnected.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, K]

Let's consider the "bottleneck" edges.
All paths from M to B can be broadly categorized into two main "channels" or branches:
1.  **The E-channel:** Paths that go through node E (e.g., M-E-B, M-L-E-B, M-E-F-G-H-B).
2.  **The J-channel:** Paths that go through node J (e.g., M-J-I-H-B, M-J-P-O-C-B).

After removing (E, B), the remaining paths from M to B are those that use the J-channel (paths 3, 4, 5, 6). All these paths originate from M via the edge (M, J). If we remove (M, J), all these paths are broken.

-   **Removing (E, B):** Breaks paths 1, 2, 7, 8, 9.
-   **Removing (M, J):** Breaks paths 3, 4, 5, 6.
All identified paths are broken. This cut set has 2 edges.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from J. I cannot remove E or J.

I will systematically identify all paths from E to J and look for critical nodes that all paths must traverse.

Let's trace the paths from E to J:

**Paths originating from E's neighbors:**

1.  **Paths through M:**
    *   E -> M -> K -> X -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   E -> M -> L -> F -> X -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   E -> M -> L -> F -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   All these paths eventually pass through G, then H, then I, and finally reach J.

2.  **Paths through F:**
    *   E -> F -> X -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   E -> F -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   These paths also eventually pass through G, then H, then I, and finally reach J.
    *   (Note: F is also reachable from M via L, but these paths are covered by the M-branch analysis.)

3.  **Paths through B:**
    *   E -> B -> H -> I -> J
    *   E -> B -> C -> I -> J
    *   E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> J
    *   E -> B -> C -> O -> Y -> J
    *   E -> B -> N -> O -> P -> J
    *   E -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J
    *   E -> B -> N -> C -> O -> P -> J
    *   E -> B -> N -> O -> Y -> J

**Identifying Bottlenecks:**

Let's analyze the "exit points" to J. J is directly connected to I, P, and Y. Therefore, any path from E to J must pass through at least one of these three nodes (I, P, or Y).

*   **Consider node I:**
    *   All paths from the M/F side (Group 1 and 2) converge on I before reaching J.
    *   Several paths from the B side also pass through I: E -> B -> H -> I -> J, E -> B -> C -> I -> J, E -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J.
    *   If I is removed, all these paths are broken.

*   **Consider nodes P and Y:**
    *   P is connected to J and O.
    *   Y is connected to J and O.
    *   All paths from E that reach J via P or Y must first pass through O. For example: E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> J, E -> B -> N -> O -> Y -> J.
    *   Therefore, O acts as a bottleneck for all paths that use P or Y to reach J. If O is removed, P and Y become isolated from E's side of the graph, effectively cutting all paths through P and Y.

**Proposed Cut Set:**

Based on this analysis, every path from E to J must pass through either node I or node O.
*   Paths like E -> ... -> I -> J are cut by removing I.
*   Paths like E -> ... -> O -> P -> J or E -> ... -> O -> Y -> J are cut by removing O.

Therefore, removing both I and O will disconnect E from J. This is a cut set of size 2: [I, O].

**Verifying Minimality:**

*   **Can we disconnect E and J by removing 1 node?**
    *   If we remove only I: Paths through O (e.g., E -> B -> C -> O -> P -> J) would still connect E and J.
    *   If we remove only O: Paths through I (e.g., E -> M -> K -> X -> G -> H -> I -> J) would still connect E and J.
    *   If we remove P: Paths through I and Y would remain.
    *   If we remove Y: Paths through I and P would remain.
    *   If we remove H: Paths through C and N (e.g., E -> B -> C -> I -> J, E -> B -> N -> O -> P -> J) would remain.
    *   No single node removal is sufficient.

Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and we found a cut set of size 2 ([I, O]), this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [I, O]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'M']}

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 3
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are L and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between L and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node L is connected to E and M.
    *   Node J is connected to M, I, P, D, and O.

2.  **Analyze paths from L to J:**
    All paths from L to J must start by traversing either the edge (L, E) or the edge (L, M).

    *   **Path 1 (via M):** L - M - J
    *   **Paths via E:** All other paths from L must begin with L - E. From E, there are multiple routes to J:
        *   L - E - B - C - I - J
        *   L - E - B - C - O - J
        *   L - E - B - N - C - I - J
        *   L - E - B - N - C - O - J
        *   L - E - B - N - Y - O - J
        *   L - E - G - H - I - J
        *   L - E - G - K - F - M - J
        *   L - E - G - F - M - J
        *   L - E - G - X - F - M - J

3.  **Consider minimum cut size:**
    *   **Can it be 1?** If I remove only one edge, say (L, M), then L can still reach J via any of the paths starting with L - E. If I remove (L, E), L can still reach J via L - M - J. If I remove any other single edge (e.g., (M, J) or (I, J)), there are still multiple alternative paths from L to J. Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect L and J. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

    *   **Can it be 2?**
        *   **Option A: Disconnect L from the rest of the graph.**
            If I remove both edges connected to L, which are (L, M) and (L, E), then L becomes an isolated node. If L is isolated, no path can exist between L and J. This set of edges has a size of 2.
            Cut Set: [(L, M), (L, E)]

        *   **Option B: Disconnect J from the rest of the graph.**
            If I remove all edges connected to J, which are (J, M), (J, I), (J, P), (J, D), and (J, O), then J becomes an isolated node. This set of edges has a size of 5. This is larger than Option A.

        *   **Option C: Other combinations of 2 edges.**
            Let's consider if there's another set of 2 edges that could disconnect L and J.
            For example, if I remove (L, E) and (M, J):
            1.  Removing (L, E) breaks all paths that start with L - E.
            2.  Removing (M, J) breaks the direct path L - M - J.
            Since all paths from L must start with either (L, E) or (L, M), and both of these initial connections are now severed from reaching J (either directly or indirectly), L is disconnected from J. This is also a valid cut set of size 2.
            Cut Set: [(L, E), (M, J)]

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and I have found multiple cut sets of size 2, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, M), (L, E)] (or [(L, E), (M, J)])

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are J (black) and E (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J and E.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between J and E:**
    To systematically approach this, I will consider the direct connections from J and the direct connections to E.
    Nodes directly connected to J: M, D, P, I.
    Nodes directly connected to E: L, B, F.

    Let's trace some distinct paths:
    *   **Path 1 (via L):** J - M - L - E
    *   **Path 2 (via B):** J - P - O - N - B - E (There are many paths to B, this is one example)
    *   **Path 3 (via F):** J - I - G - F - E (There are many paths to F, this is one example)

2.  **Check for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (the edge connectivity), I can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

    Let's examine the three paths identified above for edge-disjointness:
    *   **Path P1:** J - M - L - E
        Edges: {(J, M), (M, L), (L, E)}
    *   **Path P2:** J - P - O - N - B - E
        Edges: {(J, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B), (B, E)}
    *   **Path P3:** J - I - G - F - E
        Edges: {(J, I), (I, G), (G, F), (F, E)}

    Let's compare the edges in these three paths:
    *   (J, M) is only in P1.
    *   (M, L) is only in P1.
    *   (L, E) is only in P1.
    *   (J, P) is only in P2.
    *   (P, O) is only in P2.
    *   (O, N) is only in P2.
    *   (N, B) is only in P2.
    *   (B, E) is only in P2.
    *   (J, I) is only in P3.
    *   (I, G) is only in P3.
    *   (G, F) is only in P3.
    *   (F, E) is only in P3.

    Since there are no common edges between P1, P2, and P3, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:**
    Because I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect J and E.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect E from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to E. These edges are (L, E), (B, E), and (F, E).
    *   Removing (L, E) breaks Path P1 (J-M-L-E) and any other path ending in L-E.
    *   Removing (B, E) breaks Path P2 (J-P-O-N-B-E) and any other path ending in B-E.
    *   Removing (F, E) breaks Path P3 (J-I-G-F-E) and any other path ending in F-E.

    After removing these three edges, node E becomes isolated from all other nodes, including J. Therefore, no path can exist between J and E.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 3, and one such cut set is [(L, E), (B, E), (F, E)].

The final answer is structured as requested.

Since we have found a cut set of size 2, and we've established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes**: The two black nodes are E and I. The goal is to disconnect E from I by removing the minimum number of white nodes.

2.  **Explore Paths and Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths**: I will systematically trace paths from E to I to understand the graph's connectivity and identify vertex-disjoint paths. Finding vertex-disjoint paths helps establish a lower bound for the minimum cut set size (Menger's Theorem).

    *   **Path 1**: E - G - H - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, H}

    *   **Path 2**: E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}

    *   **Path 3**: E - M - J - P - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}

    Upon inspection, these three paths are vertex-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes.
    *   {G, H} ∩ {B, C} = {}
    *   {G, H} ∩ {M, J, P} = {}
    *   {B, C} ∩ {M, J, P} = {}

    Since there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and I is at least 3.

3.  **Identify a Candidate Cut Set of Size 3**: Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal successfully disconnects E and I. I will consider nodes that act as "bottlenecks" or "bridges" for different groups of paths.

    Let's consider the nodes J, H, and B:

    *   **Node J**: This node is a critical point for all paths originating from E via L or M.
        *   Paths like E-L-M-J-P-I, E-L-M-J-O-C-I, E-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-O-C-I all pass through J. Removing J would sever all these connections.

    *   **Node H**: This node is a critical point for all paths originating from E via F or G.
        *   Paths like E-F-X-K-G-H-I, E-F-K-G-H-I, E-F-G-H-I, E-G-H-I all pass through H. Removing H would sever all these connections.

    *   **Node B**: This node is a critical point for all paths originating directly from E via B.
        *   Paths like E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I, E-B-C-O-P-I all pass through B. Removing B would sever all these connections.

4.  **Verify the Cut Set [J, H, B]**:
    *   If J is removed, all paths from E through L or M are broken.
    *   If H is removed, all paths from E through F or G are broken.
    *   If B is removed, all paths from E through B are broken.

    Since these three nodes collectively cover all possible "entry points" from E into the rest of the graph that lead to I, removing them will ensure no path remains between E and I. Each node in the set is necessary because if any one of them is left, a path through that node's "channel" would still exist.

5.  **Conclusion**: Since we have identified 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 ([J, H, B]) that successfully disconnects E and I. Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are M (black) and B (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between M and B.

First, I identify all direct connections from M and to B:
*   M is connected to L, E, and J.
*   B is connected to N, C, H, and E.

Next, I categorize all possible paths from M to B based on the first edge taken from M:

1.  **Paths starting with M-E:**
    *   M - E - B (direct path)
    *   M - L - E - B (path through L then E)
    To break both of these paths, I can remove the edge (E, B). This single edge effectively cuts off all paths that go through node E to reach B from M.

2.  **Paths starting with M-J:**
    *   M - J - D - P - O - N - B
    *   M - J - D - P - C - B
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B
    *   M - J - P - C - B
    *   M - J - I - C - B
    *   M - J - I - H - B
    *   M - J - Y - N - B
    *   M - J - Y - O - N - B
    *   M - J - Y - O - C - B
    All these paths originate from M via the edge (M, J). The most efficient way to break all of them is to remove the edge (M, J).

Now, let's combine these observations.
If I remove the edge (E, B), all paths from M that go through E (M-E-B and M-L-E-B) are broken.
If I also remove the edge (M, J), all paths from M that go through J are broken.

Since M only has direct connections to L, E, and J, and the paths through L and E converge at E before reaching B, removing (E, B) and (M, J) will disconnect M from B.

Let's verify if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 edge) is possible:
*   If I remove only (E, B), paths through M-J-...-B still exist.
*   If I remove only (M, J), paths M-E-B and M-L-E-B still exist.
*   If I remove (M, E), paths M-L-E-B and M-J-...-B still exist.
*   If I remove (M, L), paths M-E-B and M-J-...-B still exist.
Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient.

The minimum number of edges required is 2. The cut set is [(E, B), (M, J)].
